A Lattice U(1) Chern–Simons Theory via Lattice Deligne–Beilinson Cohomology Preprint: UT-Komaba/26-2 arXiv:
Yo Ikeda

Abstract
We define Deligne–Beilinson (DB) cohomology on a cubic lattice and use it to formulate and analyze lattice U ⁡ ( 1 ) U(1) Chern–Simons theory at even levels. The continuum DB cohomology provides a refined mathematical framework for continuum U ⁡ ( 1 ) U(1) connections constructed in a patchwise manner. The lattice DB cohomology we construct retains many essential properties of the continuum DB cohomology and naturally incorporates a notion of self-linking number. The lattice U ⁡ ( 1 ) U(1) Chern–Simons action formulated using the lattice DB cohomology is expressed as a simple quadratic form via the star product, which naturally exhibits level quantization. Framed Wilson lines respecting staggered symmetry are defined in a gauge-invariant manner, and their expectation values are shown to be given by the self-linking number, as follows from completing the square. Using the lattice Hodge decomposition, we explicitly characterize the DB cohomology on a three-dimensional cubic toroidal lattice and present a gauge-fixed, rigorous path integral for the lattice Chern–Simons theory. To regulate divergences in the lattice Chern–Simons path integral arising from staggered symmetry, we introduce a small Maxwell term. The resulting error is controlled by the linear order in the small Maxwell coupling.



1
Introduction

Chern–Simons theory

In 1974, Shiing-Shen Chern and James Simons discovered Chern–Simons differential form
Chern:1974ft
. It was the beginning of the long history of Chern–Simons theory. In 1978, A.S. Schwarz first formulated Chern–Simons theory and pointed out the relationship between Chern–Simons theory and a topological invariant, which is called Ray-Singer torsion
Schwarz:1978cn
. This is now called the first example of TQFT.

Chern–Simons theory was first formulated as a Lagrangian QFT around 1980
Schonfeld:1980kb
;
Deser:1981wh
. In 1988, a connection between
U(1)
Chern–Simons theory and the linking number of knots was pointed out
Polyakov:1988md
;
Frohlich:1988
. It is so famous that Witten discovered the relationship between the
SU(2)
Chern–Simons theory and the Jones polynomial in 1989
Witten:1988hf
.

The Chern–Simons theory still plays a very important role at the cutting edge of physics today, but while the Chern–Simons theory can be formulated as an axiomatic TQFT with mathematical rigour, the continuum Chern–Simons theory as a quantum field theory of path integral still is not mathematically rigorous. From this perspective, establishing lattice Chern–Simons theory is an extremely important task.

History of lattice formulations of Chern–Simons theory

The definition of Chern–Simons theory on a three-dimensional space-time lattice has been under discussion for about 30 years, but a satisfactory formulation has only been achieved recently. In this section, we provide an overview of the process leading to the establishment of lattice Chern–Simons theory.

Chern–Simons theory on lattices began to be formulated in the early 1990s. The model known today as Dijkgraaf-Witten theory was introduced as something close to a lattice Chern-Simons theory
Dijkgraaf:1989pz
. In the same year, a naïvely discretized Chern–Simons action on a triangular lattice using the cup product of a simplicial complex was proposed in
Kavalov:1989kg
. A similar approach has been taken to formulate lattice
U(1)
Chern–Simons theory using the
U(1)
cochain formalism, namely a method that directly uses the
U(1)
link variables on the triangulation
DeMarco:2019pqv
. However, the action defined in this work has a rounding operation to the nearest integer, which makes the action a discontinuous function.

In 1992, a Chern–Simons theory on a discrete 2-dimensional + continuum 1-dimensional lattice was introduced
Eliezer:1992sq
, and the discussion of Wilson lines with nontrivial homology was advanced, but in
DeMarco:2019pqv
, it is pointed out that the theory becomes nonlocal.

The zero modes of lattice Chern–Simons theory induce difficulties for the path integral formulation. These zero modes were pointed out in
Berruto:2000dp
, and it was also pointed out that these zero modes can be eliminated by the Maxwell term. Lattice Maxwell Chern–Simons theory was also a model that had already been discussed at that time. In 1993,
Diamantini:1993iu
pointed out topological excitations as strings connecting monopoles and antimonopoles in lattice Maxwell Chern–Simons theory. For this reason, the formulation of lattice Chern–Simons theory without zero modes has also been studied. For example, a lattice Chern–Simons theory without zero modes as a determinant of the lattice Ginsparg-Wilson fermion
So:1984nf
;
Coste:1989wf
;
Bietenholz:2002mt
should be mentioned.

In 2023, a research offering a new perspective on the zero-mode problem in lattice Chern–Simons theory appeared in
Jacobson:2023cmr
. Jacobson and Sulejmanpasic constructed a lattice
U(1)
Chern–Simons theory using a method called the modified Villain formalism. While the zero modes associated with the staggered symmetries had previously been regarded as something to be removed, Jacobson and Sulejmanpasic reinterpreted them as a mechanism that imposes a framing on Wilson lines. The paper also discusses the linking number and the ’t Hooft anomaly. However, the convergence of the path integral remains unclear, and their discussion is restricted to even Chern–Simons levels.

Although the work of Jacobson and Sulejmanpasic seemed at first to provide a beautiful solution to the zero-mode problem, it is pointed out that a Maxwell term is still necessary for the convergence of the path integral
Xu:2024hyo
. Xu and Chen studied a model where a Maxwell term is added to the modified Villain
U(1)
Chern–Simons theory, showed that the partition function is finite, and gave its expression. They also analyzed several detailed properties of the theory, and discussed the case of odd Chern–Simons level
k
. They also consider Wilson lines with nontrivial homology. However, their formulation of Wilson lines differs from
Jacobson:2023cmr
, and their discussion is in infinite volume and focuses on classical solutions, so it is not fully general.

Deligne–Beilinson cohomology

Deligne–Beilinson cohomology, which at the time was called “multiplicative de Rham complex” was originally introduced by Pierre Deligne to incorporate a concept called the intermediate Jacobian into cohomology theory
Deligne:1971
. The term “multiplicative de Rham complex” is now called Deligne–Beilinson cohomology, named after the paper published by Alexander Beilinson in 1985
Beilinson:1985
.

On the other hand, the definition of Deligne–Beilinson cohomology adopted in many physics papers, including this one, is different but equivalent to Deligne’s. This definition is related to the patchwise definition of gauge fields in classical field theory. For example, consider a
U(1)
connection on a manifold
M
. A
U(1)
connection with a non-trivial Chern class can always be described by a
1
-form which satisfies the gluing condition on the good cover of
M
. The space obtained by enumerating
U(1)
gauge configurations that satisfy the gluing condition and dividing them by gauge equivalence is the Deligne–Beilinson
2
-cohomology. If we enumerate
k
-forms instead of
1
-forms and divide them by gauge equivalence, we obtain the Deligne–Beilinson
(k+1)
-cohomology. This construction is similar to a method called Čech–de Rham complex, and appears to differ from the method first proposed by Deligne. For this reason, this construction is sometimes called smooth Deligne cohomology to distinguish it from the method by Deligne. Today, it is more commonly referred to as Deligne–Beilinson cohomology without making much of a distinction.

Thus, considering a Deligne–Beilinson
2
-cohomology on a manifold is equivalent to enumerating all possible
U(1)
connections on
M
, which is the gauge-fixed path integral domain in
U(1)
gauge theory. In this sense, it is quite natural to consider a path integral on a Deligne–Beilinson 2-cohomology in
U(1)
Chern–Simons theory as well. In recent years, the formulation and properties of continuum
U(1)
Chern–Simons theory using the Deligne–Beilinson cohomology have discussed in
Guadagnini:2008bh
;
Guadagnini:2014mja
;
Thuillier:2015vma
, and the relationship between Deligne–Beilinson cohomology and a doubled lattice
U(1)
Chern–Simons theory on the lattice is pointed out in
Chen:2019mjw
.

Deligne–Beilinson
(k+1)
-cohomology is also known in physics. The construction of B-fields in string theory is well known and has also been discussed in the context of generalized symmetry
Kapustin:2014gua
;
Kapustin:2014zva
. Deligne–Beilinson cohomology of rank three or higher is often referred to as a gerbe with connection in some contexts.

From these perspectives, it seems useful to consider Deligne–Beilinson cohomology even in the formulation of lattice
U(1)
gauge theory. In 2021, Norton defined Deligne–Beilinson cohomology on simplicial complexes
Norton:2021psm
, showing that this is well-defined as a Deligne–Beilinson cohomology in discretized spacetime and that it can be discussed in the same way as continuous Deligne–Beilinson cohomology. The publication of this paper has opened the door to the formulation of lattice models using Deligne–Beilinson cohomology.

Outline of this paper

The main objectives of this paper are as follows:

•

To define the lattice Deligne--Beilinson cohomology for cubic lattices, following the (discrete) Deligne--Beilinson cohomology for triangular lattices defined in
Norton:2021psm
.

•

To define and analyze the lattice
U(1)
Chern–Simons theory on the
3
d cubic toroidal lattice, referring to the method of the continuum
U(1)
Chern–Simons theory pointed out in
Guadagnini:2008bh
;
Thuillier:2015vma
.

This paper is divided into three parts.
Part
I
is a review part. In Section
2
, we briefly review the Deligne–Beilinson formulation of continuum
U(1)
Chern–Simons theory discussed in
Guadagnini:2008bh
;
Thuillier:2015vma
, and in Section
3
, we briefly review the modified Villain formulation of lattice
U(1)
Chern–Simons theory discussed in
Jacobson:2023cmr
;
Xu:2024hyo
.

In Part
II
, we give a definition of the lattice DB cohomology. In Section
4
and
5
, we define the Deligne–Beilinson cohomology for the
d
-dimensional toroidal lattice. This discussion shows that the methodology used in
Norton:2021psm
to discuss the Deligne–Beilinson cohomology for the triangulated lattice can be similarly applied to the
d
-dimensional cubic toroidal lattice. In Section
6
, we define the star product, which is an important operation in Deligne–Beilinson cohomology theory. In Section
7
, we define gauge-invariant integrals in Deligne–Beilinson cohomology. The gauge-invariant half-integer integral introduced in Section
7
shows that the definition of Wilson line in
Jacobson:2023cmr
can also be defined within lattice Deligne–Beilinson theory. In Section
8
, we analyze aspects of the Deligne–Beilinson cohomology as a differential cohomology, which can be used to define gauge-fixed path integrals. In Section
9
, we discuss the relationship between star products and knot theory in the three-dimensional cubic toroidal lattice. The mod
2k
linking number is a particularly interesting topic, because it is where the geometric ideas of knot theory and Deligne–Beilinson cohomology come together.

Part
III
is one of the main topics of this paper. In Section
10
, we define
U(1)
Chern–Simons theory on a three-dimensional toroidal lattice using the lattice Deligne–Beilinson cohomology and discuss its properties. This discussion shows that the method of continuum Chern–Simons theory
Guadagnini:2008bh
;
Thuillier:2015vma
, formulated in terms of continuous Deligne–Beilinson cohomology, can also be applied to Chern–Simons theory on a lattice. In Section
11
, we demonstrate that lattice Maxwell–Chern–Simons (MCS) theory has a finite partition function, and the expectation values of Wilson lines appear as linking numbers and self-linking numbers, and have the properties expected from continuum Chern–Simons theory. While the expectation values calculated in lattice Maxwell–Chern–Simons theory contain some error, we show that the error can be controlled in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
, where
\epsilon
denotes the coefficient of the Maxwell term.

Finally, we discuss some applications. In Section
12
, we briefly review the non-invertible chiral transformation defect in continuum massless QED, and then discuss how the same defect can be constructed using lattice Maxwell–Chern–Simons theory. In Section
13
, we discuss other applications, and we summarize our results and give the outlook in Section
14
.

Part I
Review

2
Continuous Chern–Simons theory formulated using DB cohomology

In this section, we define continuous Deligne–Beilinson (DB) cohomology and provide a very brief review of the properties of
U(1)
Chern–Simons theory defined from it. The content of this section is based on
Guadagnini:2008bh
;
Thuillier:2015vma
.

2.1
DB
2
-cohomology on
3
-manifolds

For simplicity, we will only discuss DB 2-cohomology in this review. For a more general definition, see
Guadagnini:2008bh
;
Thuillier:2015vma
.

Let
M
be a compact three-dimensional manifold. We fix a finite good open cover of
M

\{U_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in I}
. We introduce an abbreviation

\displaystyle U_{\alpha\beta}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}&(\alpha\neq\beta)\\
\emptyset&(\mathrm{otherwise})\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle U_{\alpha\beta\gamma}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}\cap U_{\gamma}&(\alpha\neq\beta\neq\gamma\neq\alpha)\\
\emptyset&(\mathrm{otherwise})\end{array}\right..

The Deligne–Beilinson 2-cocycles on
M
, denoted
Z^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
,
1
1

1

Since the top form is the
1
-form, it is sometimes written by
Z^{1}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
, but this is inconsistent with the star product described below. In this paper, we respect consistency with the product and write it as
Z^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
.
are given by

Z^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)=\Bigl\{\bigl(\{A_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in I}\;,\;\{\Lambda_{\alpha\beta}\}_{\alpha,\beta\in I}\;,\;\{n_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in I}\bigr)\Bigr\},

(5)

where
A,\Lambda,n
are

\displaystyle A_{\alpha}\in\Omega^{1}(U_{\alpha}),

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\alpha\beta}\in\Omega^{0}(U_{\alpha\beta}),

\displaystyle n_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\in\Omega^{-1}(U_{\alpha\beta\gamma}),

(6)

that satisfy

\displaystyle(\delta A)_{\alpha\beta}=A_{\beta}-A_{\alpha}=d\Lambda_{\alpha\beta},

\displaystyle(\delta\Lambda)_{\alpha\beta\gamma}=\Lambda_{\beta\gamma}+\Lambda_{\gamma\alpha}+\Lambda_{\alpha\beta}=-d_{-1}n_{\alpha\beta\gamma}.

(7)

\Omega^{n}(U)
is the set of differential
n
-forms on
U
, and we interpret a
(-1)
-form that belongs to
\Omega^{-1}(U_{\alpha\beta\gamma})
as an assignment of an integer
to
U_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
. The operator
d_{-1}
is defined to regard such a
(-1)
-form as a
\mathbb{Z}
-valued function on
U_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
. We define the addition operation on
Z^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
as the sum of each component. For simplicity, we omit the subscripts and denote

\bigl(\{A_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in I}\;,\;\{\Lambda_{\alpha\beta}\}_{\alpha,\beta\in I}\;,\;\{n_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in I}\bigr)\quad\text{by}\quad(A,\Lambda,n).

There are two types of gauge transformation for the element of Deligne–Beilinson 2-cocycle
(A,\Lambda,n)
. The ordinary gauge transformation with its parameter function

\{\xi_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in I}\;,\;\xi_{\alpha}\in\Omega^{0}(U_{\alpha})

(8)

is described by

\displaystyle A_{\alpha}\to A_{\alpha}+d\xi_{\alpha},

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\alpha\beta}\to\Lambda_{\alpha\beta}+(\delta\xi)_{\alpha\beta}.

(9)

It is clear that this transformation sends a DB 2-cocycle to a DB 2-cocycle. On the other hand, a large gauge transformation with its parameter function

\{m_{\alpha\beta}\}_{\alpha,\beta\in I}\;,\;\xi_{\alpha}\in\Omega^{-1}(U_{\alpha})

(10)

is defined by

\displaystyle\Lambda\to\Lambda-m,

\displaystyle n\to n+\delta m.

(11)

We can also see that this transformation sends a DB 2-cocycle to a DB 2-cocycle.

Let us define Deligne–Beilinson 2-cohomology
H^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
as a quotient of
Z^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
divided by the equivalence relation
\sim
generated by these two gauge transformations. Written in mathematical form,

H^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)=Z^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)/\sim.

(12)

We adopt the notation
[(A,\Lambda,n)]
to denote the element of
H^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
using the natural projection
[\bullet]
of the quotient space, which is consistent with the addition operation on
Z^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
.

H^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
has a gauge-invariant line integral. For closed curve
\gamma:S^{1}\to M
, we can arbitrarily take a direct sum decomposition of
\gamma

\gamma=\gamma_{\alpha_{1}}+s_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}+\gamma_{\alpha_{2}}+s_{\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}}+\dots+\gamma_{\alpha_{N}}+s_{\alpha_{N}\alpha_{1}}

(13)

that satisfies

\displaystyle\gamma_{\alpha_{i}}\subset U_{\alpha_{i}},

\displaystyle\gamma_{\alpha_{i}}\cong\;]0,1[\,,

\displaystyle s_{\alpha_{j}\alpha_{k}}\in U_{\alpha_{j}\alpha_{k}},

(14)

where the notation
]0,1[
denotes the open interval. Then the line integral of
(A,\Lambda,n)
along
\gamma
can be well-defined by

\int_{\gamma}(A,\Lambda,n)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\gamma_{i}}A_{\alpha_{i}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Lambda_{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i+1}}(s_{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i+1}}).

(15)

The value of the line integration is modulo-
\mathbb{Z}
invariant under the choice of the decomposition of
\gamma
and the two gauge transformations of
(A,\Lambda,n)
. Therefore, the line integral can be said to be
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
valued gauge-invariant integral.

At the end of this section, let us mention the relation between
U(1)
connection on classical field theory and DB
2
-cohomology. The patchwise
U(1)
connection defined in common textbooks such as
Nakahara:2003nw
is exactly equivalent to the Deligne–Beilinson
2
-cocycle, and its gauge fixing is equivalent to the Deligne–Beilinson 2-cohomology. The only difference is that
\frac{1}{2\pi i}\log g_{\alpha\beta}=\Lambda_{\alpha\beta}
is used instead of the usual
U(1)
transition function
g_{\alpha\beta}
, and
n
corresponds to the
2\pi i
ambiguity in
\log
that arises when defining
\Lambda
. The gauge-invariant line integral of the DB cocycle corresponds to the line integral of the
U(1)
connection, i.e., the Wilson line. Similar to the definition of
F=dA
in
U(1)
gauge theory, the curvature
dA
of the Deligne–Beilinson cocycle is defined as a global
2
-form by the gluing condition, and its period is always an integer.

2.2
Star product in continuum DB cohomology

The star product is a bilinear mapping
\star:Z^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)\times Z^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)\to Z^{4}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
. Actually,
\star:H^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)\times H^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)\to H^{4}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
also makes sense because the star product is consistent with gauge equivalence.
Z^{4}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
is a certain set of
5
-tuples whose elements satisfy certain gluing conditions, and
H^{4}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
can be defined by dividing
Z^{4}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
by the equivalence relation given by gauge transformations and also has a gauge-invariant
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
-valued integral along
M
. We omit further discussions about the definition of
H^{4}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
.

Let us consider two DB cocycles
\bm{\omega}=(\omega^{(1,0)},\omega^{(0,1)},\omega^{(-1,2)})
and
\bm{\eta}=(\eta^{(1,0)},\eta^{(0,1)},\eta^{(-1,2)})
. The star product of these two DB cocycles
\bm{\omega}\star\bm{\eta}
is defined by

\begin{split}&\bm{\omega}\star\bm{\eta}\\
=&(\omega^{(1,0)}_{\alpha}\wedge d\eta^{(1,0)}_{\alpha},\omega^{(0,1)}_{\alpha\beta}\wedge d\eta^{(1,0)}_{\beta},\omega^{(-1,2)}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\wedge\eta^{(1,0)}_{\gamma},\omega^{(-1,2)}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\wedge\eta^{(0,1)}_{\gamma\rho},\omega^{(-1,2)}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\wedge\eta^{(-1,2)}_{\gamma\rho\sigma}).\end{split}

(16)

Since the product is consistent with the projection map,

\begin{split}&[\bm{\omega}]\star[\bm{\eta}]=[\bm{\omega}\star\bm{\eta}]\\
=&[(\omega^{(1,0)}_{\alpha}\wedge d\eta^{(1,0)}_{\alpha},\omega^{(0,1)}_{\alpha\beta}\wedge d\eta^{(1,0)}_{\beta},\omega^{(-1,2)}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\wedge\eta^{(1,0)}_{\gamma},\omega^{(-1,2)}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\wedge\eta^{(0,1)}_{\gamma\rho},\omega^{(-1,2)}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\wedge\eta^{(-1,2)}_{\gamma\rho\sigma})]\end{split}

(17)

also makes sense.

The star product on
H^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
has two important properties: (graded) commutativity
2
2

2

The product of
H^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
is simply commutative because
(-1)^{2\cdot 2}=1
.
and Pontrjagin duality. Pontrjagin duality is the property that there exists a Pontrjagin dual
\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}\in H^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
such that

\int_{\gamma}\bm{\omega}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}}}{{=}}\int_{M}\bm{\omega}\star\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}\qquad(\text{for all $\bm{\omega}\in H^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)$})

(18)

for any closed curve
\gamma
, where
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}}}{{=}}
means modulo
\mathbb{Z}
equality.

In the continuous DB formalism, it is said that the Pontrjagin dual
\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}
“exists” for all closed curves
\gamma
; however, that is not actually an accurate claim. It shares a similar problem for the formulation of chainwise Poincaré duality on de Rham cohomology theory. The chainwise Poincaré duality is mathematically defined as a de Rham current, which is a counterpart of the Schwartz distribution in de Rham theory. For similar reasons, a distributional formulation should be applied to the definition of Pontrjagin duality, but we will skip a detailed discussion here.

2.3
Continuum Chern–Simons theory via DB cohomology

We define level
2k
Chern–Simons theory on
M
by

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{CS}}[[\bm{a}]]=2\pi k\int_{M}[\bm{a}]\star[\bm{a}],

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}=\int_{[\bm{a}]\in H^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)}\mathcal{D}[\bm{a}]\exp\{iS_{\mathrm{CS}}[[\bm{a}]]\}.

(19)

The gauge invariance of the action and the
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
-valuedness of the integral require
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. There is no gauge redundancy in the path integral because we set the integral region as
[\bm{a}]\in H^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
and
H^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)
is already divided by gauge equivalence. We assume path integral measure
\mathcal{D}[\bm{a}]
is invariant under shift transformation
[\bm{a}]\to[\bm{a}+\bm{\omega}]
.

Next, let us discuss the properties of the Chern–Simons theory we defined. For simplicity, we only consider the case
M=\mathbb{T}^{3}
. We should formulate the
\mathbb{Z}_{2k}
global
1
-form symmetry that Chern–Simons theory must have. Let each
\beta_{23},\beta_{31},\beta_{12}\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
be a chainwise Poincaré dual of
e_{23},e_{31},e_{12},
which is the basis of
H_{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{Z})
.
3
3

3

As mentioned in Section
2.2
,
\beta
should be defined as a de Rham current, so it is actually inappropriate to write
\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
.
Defining
\delta_{-1}
by

\delta_{-1}:\Omega^{1}(M)\ni\omega\mapsto\{(\delta_{-1}\omega)_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in I}=\{\omega|_{U_{\alpha}}\}_{\alpha\in I},

(20)

we find
(\delta_{-1}\omega,0,0)
is a DB 2-cocycle on
M
for all
\omega\in\Omega^{1}(M)
. We can define
\mathbb{Z}_{2k}
global
1
-form symmetry by

[\bm{a}]\to\left[\bm{a}+\frac{\bm{\beta}_{ij}}{2k}\right],

(21)

where

\frac{\bm{\beta}_{ij}}{2k}=\left(\frac{1}{2k}\delta_{-1}\beta_{ij},0,0\right)\in Z^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)

(22)

and the level
2k
Chern–Simons action is invariant under this transformation. In fact, from bilinearity of
\star
and (graded) commutativity, the action changes as follows:

k\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\bm{a}\star\bm{a}\to k\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\bm{a}\star\bm{a}+2k\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\bm{a}\star\frac{\bm{\beta}_{ij}}{2k}+k\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\frac{\bm{\beta}_{ij}}{2k}\star\frac{\bm{\beta}_{ij}}{2k}.

(23)

Referring to
d\beta_{ij}=0
and the definition of
\star
, we can see the second term is an integer, and the third term is
0
. Therefore,
S_{\mathrm{CS}}
is certainly modulo-
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
invariant under the shift
\bm{a}\to\bm{a}+\bm{\beta}/2k
.

2.4
Expectation values of Wilson lines

The expectation value of the Wilson line
\gamma
, denoted
\langle W(\gamma)\rangle
, is defined by

\begin{split}\langle W(\gamma)\rangle&=\mathcal{Z}^{-1}\int_{[\bm{a}]\in H^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)}\mathcal{D}[\bm{a}]\exp\left[iS_{\mathrm{CS}}[[\bm{a}]]+2\pi i\int_{\gamma}[\bm{a}]\right]\\
&=\mathcal{Z}^{-1}\int_{[\bm{a}]\in H^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)}\mathcal{D}[\bm{a}]\exp\left[2\pi ik\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}[\bm{a}]\star[\bm{a}]+2\pi i\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}[\bm{a}]\star[\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}]\right].\end{split}

(24)

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2k}

1
-form symmetry of the action leads the transformation of the path integral variable

[\bm{a}]\to\left[\bm{a}+\frac{\bm{\beta}_{ij}}{2k}\right],

(25)

and we obtain

\langle W(\gamma)\rangle=\exp\left[2\pi i\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\left[\frac{\bm{\beta}_{ij}}{2k}\right]\star[\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}]\right]\langle W(\gamma)\rangle.

(26)

If there is
\bm{\beta}_{ij}
that satisfies

\exp\left[2\pi i\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\left[\frac{\bm{\beta}_{ij}}{2k}\right]\star[\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}]\right]\neq 1,

(27)

then,
\langle W(\gamma)\rangle=0
is concluded. To require the Wilson line to have a nontrivial expectation value, we should impose a condition

\forall\bm{\beta}_{ij},\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\left[\frac{\bm{\beta}_{ij}}{2k}\right]\star[\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}]\in\mathbb{Z}\;\biggl(\Longleftrightarrow\;[\gamma]\in H_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3};2k\mathbb{Z})\biggr)

(28)

to the Wilson line
\gamma
. When the condition
[\gamma]\in H_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3};2k\mathbb{Z})
is satisfied, a division
[\frac{\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}}{2k}]
can be well-defined.
4
4

4

A more careful discussion is actually required for this argument; see also Section
9.6
.
Then we can complete the square as follows:

\begin{split}&\langle W(\gamma)\rangle\\
=&\mathcal{Z}^{-1}\int_{[\bm{a}]\in H^{2}_{\mathrm{DB}}(M)}\mathcal{D}[\bm{a}]\exp\Biggl[2\pi ik\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\biggl\{\left([\bm{a}]+\left[\frac{\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}}{2k}\right]\right)\star\left([\bm{a}]+\left[\frac{\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}}{2k}\right]\right)-\left[\frac{\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}}{2k}\right]\star\left[\frac{\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}}{2k}\right]\biggr\}\Biggr]\\
=&\exp\left[-2\pi ik\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\left[\frac{\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}}{2k}\right]\star\left[\frac{\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}}{2k}\right]\right].\end{split}

(29)

As a consequence, we find

\langle W(\gamma)\rangle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\displaystyle\exp\left[-2\pi ik\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\left[\frac{\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}}{2k}\right]\star\left[\frac{\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}}{2k}\right]\right]&(\gamma\in H_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3};2k\mathbb{Z}))\\
&\\
0&(\gamma\notin H_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3};2k\mathbb{Z}))\end{array}\right..

(30)

The integral
k\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\left[\frac{\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}}{2k}\right]\star\left[\frac{\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}}{2k}\right]
is a
(\frac{1}{2k}\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}
-valued topological number, and is equal to the
\mathrm{mod}\,2k
linking number
5
5

5

Actually, there is an ambiguity in the distributional definition of
\bm{\eta}_{\gamma}
, and all
\mathrm{mod}\,4k
self-linking numbers that should appear are manually set to 0. See
Guadagnini:2008bh
;
Thuillier:2015vma
for details.
defined later in Section
9.6
.

3
Lattice Chern–Simons theory formulated using the modified Villain formalism

In this section, we will give a very brief review of the modified Villain formalism construction of lattice Chern–Simons theory based on the papers
Jacobson:2023cmr
;
Xu:2024hyo
.

3.1
Modified Villain formalism

We consider a three-dimensional cubic lattice without boundary. In the Villain formalism, the
U(1)
gauge field is constructed by
\mathbb{R}
-valued link variable
a
and
\mathbb{Z}
-valued plaquette variable
n
. These variables transform under ordinary gauge transformations as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}a\to a+d\lambda\\
n\to n\end{array}\right.,

(31)

and under large gauge transformations as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}a\to a+2\pi m\\
n\to n+dm\end{array}\right.,

(32)

where
\mathbb{R}
-valued site variable
\lambda
and
\mathbb{Z}
-valued link variable
m
are the parameter functions of these gauge transformations.

The most naïve lattice Chern–Simons action might be

\sum_{c}\frac{ik}{4\pi}a\smallsmile da,

(33)

where
\smallsmile
is the cup product on cubic lattice, and
\sum_{c}
means the sum over
3
d cubes on the lattice. This action changes by

\sum_{c}\frac{ik}{2}[m\smallsmile da+a\smallsmile dm+2\pi m\smallsmile dm]

(34)

under the large gauge transformation. To cancel the first and second term, let us try to add a term
-2\pi(a\smallsmile n+n\smallsmile a)
to the Lagrangian. Then the improved action becomes

\sum_{c}\frac{ik}{4\pi}[a\smallsmile da-2\pi(a\smallsmile n+n\smallsmile a)].

(35)

While this action is not invariant under ordinary gauge transformation, in the case
dn=0
, this action is invariant under the ordinary gauge transformation. Thus, we keep the symmetry by imposing
dn=0
.
To enforce this condition, we introduce a Lagrange-multiplier site variable
\varphi
and add the term

i\sum_{c}\varphi\smallsmile dn

(36)

to the action. The action then becomes

\sum_{c}\left\{\frac{ik}{4\pi}\bigl[a\smallsmile da-2\pi(a\smallsmile n+n\smallsmile a)\bigr]+i\,\varphi\smallsmile dn\right\}.

(37)

Now that the action has the term
\varphi\smallsmile dn
, we can implement off-shell ordinary gauge invariance. The action changes by

\sum_{c}\frac{ik}{4\pi}[d(\lambda\smallsmile da)-2\pi(d\lambda\smallsmile n+n\smallsmile d\lambda)]=\sum_{c}\frac{ik}{4\pi}[-2\pi(\lambda\smallsmile dn+dn\smallsmile\lambda)]

(38)

under (ordinary) gauge transformation. We require that
\varphi
changes under ordinary gauge transformation as follows:

\varphi\;\to\;\varphi-k\lambda.

(39)

Then the action changes by

\sum_{c}\frac{ik}{4\pi}\bigl[-2\pi(-\lambda\smallsmile dn+dn\smallsmile\lambda)\bigr]\;=\;\sum_{c}-\frac{ik}{2}\,d\lambda\smallsmile_{1}dn

(40)

under ordinary gauge transformation. Adding
-\frac{ik}{2}a\smallsmile_{1}dn
, the action becomes

S[a,n,\varphi]=\sum_{c}\left\{\frac{ik}{4\pi}[a\smallsmile da-2\pi(a\smallsmile n+n\smallsmile a)]-\frac{ik}{2}a\smallsmile_{1}dn+i\varphi\smallsmile dn\right\},

(41)

and the (off-shell ordinary) gauge invariance is realized. Since this action changes by
i\pi k\mathbb{Z}
under a large gauge transformation,
k\in 2\mathbb{Z}
guarantees the large gauge invariance of the action. This means that the Chern–Simons level
k
is restricted to even integers in this formulation.
6
6

6

It is known that adding Majorana fermion action can make the odd level Chern–Simons action well-defined. See
Xu:2024hyo
for details.
We can thus define the lattice Chern–Simons theory

\mathcal{Z}=(\mathrm{gauge}\;\mathrm{vol.})^{-1}\int\mathcal{D}a\mathcal{D}n\mathcal{D}\varphi\;e^{S[a,n,\varphi]}.

(42)

We will not discuss the gauge fixing and the convergence of the path integral.
7
7

7

For the path integral with the introduction of Maxwell terms and appropriate gauge fixing, see Section 2.3 and Chapter 5 in
Xu:2024hyo
.

3.2
Staggered symmetry and Wilson line

We first consider a transformation
A\to A+\epsilon
to explore
1
-form symmetries of the action. The change of the action (
41
) equals to

\sum_{c}\left[\epsilon\smallsmile\left(da-2\pi n+\frac{1}{2}d\epsilon\right)+\left(da-2\pi n+\frac{1}{2}d\epsilon\right)\smallsmile\epsilon-2\pi\epsilon\smallsmile_{1}dn\right].

(43)

Integrating
\varphi
yields
dn=0
and the third term vanishes, and if there are several
1
-form
\epsilon
which satisfy

\sum_{c}\left(\epsilon\smallsmile X+X\smallsmile\epsilon\right)=0\qquad(\mathrm{for}\;\mathrm{all}\;X\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})),

(44)

the transformation
A\to A+\epsilon
can be considered as a
1
-form symmetry of the action. Such an
\epsilon
should satisfy

\epsilon(x,\mu)=-\epsilon(x+(1,1,1),\mu)\qquad(\text{for all $x,\mu$}),

(45)

and we can construct it. Let us call this type of symmetry staggered symmetry.

The Wilson line has to be invariant under the staggered symmetry to have a nontrivial expectation value. The ordinary definition of the Wilson line

\exp\left[i\int_{\gamma}A\right]

(46)

is generally not invariant under staggered symmetries. Then we first prepare a closed line
\tilde{\gamma}
on the dual lattice, splitting one line into two half lines, and define the framed Wilson line operator

\exp\left[\frac{i}{2}\int_{\tilde{\gamma}_{+}}A+\frac{i}{2}\int_{\tilde{\gamma}_{-}}A\right]=\exp\left[\frac{i}{2}\sum_{c}(A\smallsmile\mathrm{PD}(\tilde{\gamma})+\mathrm{PD}(\tilde{\gamma})\smallsmile A)\right]

(47)

instead of an ordinary one. Note that
\tilde{\gamma}_{+}
(resp.
\tilde{\gamma}_{-}
) means
\frac{1}{2}\hat{1}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{2}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{3}
(resp.
-\frac{1}{2}\hat{1}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{2}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{3}
) translation of
\tilde{\gamma}
, and
\mathrm{PD}(\tilde{\gamma})
denotes a Poincaré dual of
\tilde{\gamma}
, defined by

\begin{split}&\mathrm{PD}(\tilde{\gamma})(x;\mu,\nu)\\
=&(\text{the signed number of times that $\tilde{\gamma}$ passes through the plaquette $(x;\mu,\nu)$}).\end{split}

(48)

However, (
47
) is still imperfect because it lacks the large gauge invariance. Adding a higher cup term to the imperfect definition, we complete the definition

W(\tilde{\gamma})=\exp\left[\frac{i}{2}\sum_{c}(A\smallsmile\mathrm{PD}(\tilde{\gamma})+\mathrm{PD}(\tilde{\gamma})\smallsmile A)-i\pi\sum_{c}\mathrm{PD}(\tilde{\gamma})\smallsmile_{1}n\right].

(49)

This definition respects both staggered symmetry and gauge symmetry. As we have seen, staggered symmetry, which was thought to be removed, is reinterpreted by Jacobson and Sulejmanpasic as a framing of the Wilson line.

Let us briefly comment on the expectation value of the Wilson line. As seen in (
42
), the path integral of the partition function is difficult to calculate, and the convergence of the path integral seems ambiguous. Jacobson and Sulejmanpasic introduced a background field of
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
symmetry to circumvent this problem, and they proved the two following properties of the Wilson line. First, consider a closed curve
\tilde{\gamma}
on the dual lattice. Let
\tilde{\gamma}_{\mathrm{twist}}
be a locally deformed curve whose self-linking number increases by
l
. The expectation values of these two lines have a relation

\langle W(\tilde{\gamma}_{\mathrm{twist}})\rangle=\exp\left(\frac{2\pi il}{2k}\right)\langle W(\tilde{\gamma})\rangle.

(50)

In addition, let
\tilde{\gamma},\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}
be two closed curves which have linking number
l
. Then we have

\langle W(\tilde{\gamma})W(\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime})\rangle=\exp\left(\frac{2\pi il}{k}\right)\langle W(\tilde{\gamma})\rangle\langle W(\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime})\rangle.

(51)

These properties implemented on the lattice are the same as the corresponding properties of the Wilson line in continuum level
k
Chern–Simons theory.

Part II
The lattice DB cohomology on
d
-dim cubic torus

We move on to the main topic of this paper. In this part, we define the lattice Deligne–Beilinson cohomology on a
d
-dimensional cubic toroidal lattice. The method used to define the lattice DB cohomology in this part follows the approach outlined in
Norton:2021psm
, but various details of the construction are completed in the present work.

4
Lattice DB cochain

Let
L
be a positive integer. We consider the cubic toroidal lattice of volume
L^{d}
. We define
\mathbb{L}^{d}
as a set of grid points

\mathbb{L}^{d}=\{0,1,\ldots,L-1\}^{d}\cong(\mathbb{Z}/L\mathbb{Z})^{d}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{d}/L\mathbb{Z}^{d},

(52)

and
\tilde{\mathbb{L}}^{d}
as a set of dual grid points

\tilde{\mathbb{L}}^{d}=\left\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2},\ldots,\frac{2L-1}{2}\right\}^{d}\cong(\mathbb{Z}+1/2)^{d}/L\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

(53)

4.1
Lattice Čech–de Rham chain

Let
x_{+\dots+}
be an abbreviation for
x+\frac{1}{2}\hat{1}+\dots+\frac{1}{2}\hat{d}
for each point
x\in\mathbb{L}^{d}
, where
\hat{i}=(0,\ldots,1,\ldots,0)\;\;(\text{only the $i$-th element is $1$})
.

We define a
d
-dimensional cube with sides of length one and centered at point
x_{+\dots+}
, and denote it by
\mathrm{cube}(x_{+\dots+})
. This cube is regarded as a subset of the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{d}/L\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and is given by

\mathrm{cube}(x_{+\dots+}):=\mathrm{pr}\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\middle|\|x_{+\dots+}-y\|_{\mathrm{max}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\right\},

(54)

where
\mathrm{pr}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{T}^{d}
is natural projection on quotient space
\mathbb{R}^{d}/L\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

For
0\leq i\leq d\;,\;-1\leq j\leq d
,
8
8

8

Note that when
j=-1
,
(\tilde{\mathbb{L}}^{d})^{j+1}
is the empty direct product and can be understood as a one point set
\{\ast\}
and
\mathrm{cube}(\tilde{x}^{(0)})\cap\cdots\cap\mathrm{cube}(\tilde{x}^{(j)})
is identified with
\mathbb{T}^{d}
itself. In any case,
\mathrm{cube}_{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})
is well-defined even in
j=-1
.
we define a set of lattice Čech–de Rham cube of degree
(i,j)
, denoted
\mathrm{cube}_{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})
, by

\begin{split}\mathrm{cube}_{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d}):=&\Bigl\{(x\;;\;\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{i}\;;\;\tilde{x}^{(0)},\dots,\tilde{x}^{(j)})\in\mathbb{L}^{d}\times\{1,\dots,d\}^{i}\times(\tilde{\mathbb{L}}^{d})^{j+1}\\
&\mid 0\leq\;\;\forall\lambda_{1},\dots,\forall\lambda_{i}\;\;\leq 1,\\
&(x+\lambda_{1}\hat{\mu}_{1}+\dots\lambda_{i}\hat{\mu}_{i})/L\mathbb{Z}^{d}\in\mathrm{cube}(\tilde{x}^{(0)})\cap\cdots\cap\mathrm{cube}(\tilde{x}^{(j)})\Bigr\},\end{split}

(55)

and we extend the definition for
i=-1\;,\;-1\leq j\leq d
by

\begin{split}\mathrm{cube}_{(-1,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d}):=&\Bigl\{(\ast\;;\;\ast\;;\;\tilde{x}^{(0)},\dots,\tilde{x}^{(j)})\in\{\ast\}\times\{\ast\}\times(\tilde{\mathbb{L}}^{d})^{j+1}\\
&\mid\mathrm{cube}(\tilde{x}^{(0)})\cap\cdots\cap\mathrm{cube}(\tilde{x}^{(j)})\neq\emptyset\Bigr\},\end{split}

(56)

where
\{\ast\}
is the one point set. Figures
2
–
8
provide concrete examples and illustrations of lattice Čech–de Rham cubes on a two-dimensional cubic lattice
\mathbb{L}^{2}
.

For
-1\leq i\leq d\;,\;-1\leq j\leq d
, we define lattice Čech–de Rham chain
C_{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})
of degree
(i,j)
by

C_{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d}):=\left(\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathrm{cube}_{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d}))\right)/\sim,

(57)

where
\sim
is an equivalence relation of antisymmetry under permutations of the
\mu
’s

\begin{array}[]{llllll}\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{i},&&&&&\\
&(x\;;&\;\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{i}&;&\;\tilde{x}^{(0)},\dots,\tilde{x}^{(j)}&)\\
\sim\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)&(x\;;&\;\mu_{\sigma(1)},\dots,\mu_{\sigma(i)}&;&\;\tilde{x}^{(0)},\dots,\tilde{x}^{(j)}&),\end{array}

(58)

and of antisymmetry under permutations of the
\tilde{x}
’s

\begin{array}[]{llllll}\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{j+1},&&&&&\\
&(x\;;&\;\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{i}&;&\;\tilde{x}^{(0)},\dots,\tilde{x}^{(j)}&)\\
\sim\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)&(x\;;&\;\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{i}&;&\;\tilde{x}^{(\sigma(0))},\dots,\tilde{x}^{(\sigma(j))}&).\end{array}

(59)

Figure 1:
Examples of elements of
\mathrm{cube}_{(0,0)}(\mathbb{L}^{2})

Figure 2:
Examples of elements of
\mathrm{cube}_{(1,0)}(\mathbb{L}^{2})

Figure 3:
An example of an element of
\mathrm{cube}_{(2,0)}(\mathbb{L}^{2})

Figure 4:
Examples of elements of
\mathrm{cube}_{(0,1)}(\mathbb{L}^{2})

Figure 5:
Another example of an element of
\mathrm{cube}_{(0,1)}(\mathbb{L}^{2})

Figure 6:
An example of an element of
\mathrm{cube}_{(1,1)}(\mathbb{L}^{2})

Figure 7:
An example of an element of
\mathrm{cube}_{(0,2)}(\mathbb{L}^{2})

Figure 8:
An example of an element of
\mathrm{cube}_{(0,3)}(\mathbb{L}^{2})

4.2
Lattice Čech–de Rham cochain

For
0\leq i\leq d\;,\;-1\leq j\leq d
, lattice Čech–de Rham cochain of degree
(i,j)
, denoted
C^{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})
, is defined by

C^{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d}):=\{\omega:C_{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}\mid\text{$\omega$ is $\mathbb{Z}$-linear}\},

(60)

or equivalently,

\begin{split}C^{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d}):=\{&\omega:\mathrm{cube}_{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}\mid\text{$\omega$ is antisymmetric under}\\
&\text{ permutations of $\{1,\ldots,d\}^{i}$ and permutations of $(\tilde{\mathbb{L}}^{3})^{j+1}$}\}.\end{split}

(61)

We extend the definition of
C^{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})
for
i=-1\;,\;-1\leq j\leq d
by

C^{(-1,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d}):=\{\omega:C_{(-1,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\to\mathbb{Z}\mid\text{$\omega$ is $\mathbb{Z}$-linear}\},

(62)

or equivalently,

\begin{split}C^{(-1,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d}):=\{&\omega:\mathrm{cube}_{(-1,j)}\to\mathbb{Z}\\
&\mid\text{$\omega$ is antisymmetric under permutations of $(\tilde{\mathbb{L}}^{3})^{j+1}$}\}.\end{split}

(63)

However, for
C^{(-1,j)}
, we take the range of
\omega
to be
\mathbb{Z}
for later convenience. It should be emphasized that this convention is crucial throughout the entire paper.

We will write the arguments of
\omega^{(i,j)}\in C^{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})
with two semicolons separating the three sets of variables: for example,

\displaystyle\omega^{(i,j)}(x\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{i}\;;\;\tilde{x}^{(0)},\ldots,\tilde{x}^{(j)}),

\displaystyle\omega^{(-1,j)}(\;;\;\;;\;\tilde{x}^{(0)},\ldots,\tilde{x}^{(j)}).

(64)

The cochain we have defined above has
\tilde{\mathbb{L}}^{d}
as a variable, which corresponds to the indices of patches in the definition of a patchwise gauge field (
6
). That is, we consider each unit cube of the lattice as a patch, and such a method is already widely used
Luscher:1981zq
;
Abe:2023ncy
.

4.3
Coboundary operations

4.3.1
Čech coboundary operation

For
-1\leq j\leq d-1
, we introduce
j
-th Čech coboundary operation
\delta_{j}:C^{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\to C^{(i,j+1)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})
by

(\delta_{j}\omega)^{(i,j+1)}(\bullet\;;\;\bullet\;;\;\tilde{x}^{(0)},\ldots,\tilde{x}^{(j+1)})=\sum_{k=0}^{j+1}(-1)^{k}\omega^{(i,j)}(\bullet\;;\;\bullet\;;\;\tilde{x}^{(0)},\ldots,\check{\tilde{x}}^{(k)},\ldots,\tilde{x}^{(j+1)}),

(65)

where
\check{\tilde{x}}^{(k)}
means eliminating
\tilde{x}^{(k)}
.

Even for
j=-1
,
(-1)
-th Čech coboundary operation
\delta_{-1}:\Omega^{i}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\to C^{(i,0)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})
can be defined by

(\delta_{-1}\omega)(\bullet\;;\;\bullet\;;\;\tilde{x})=\omega^{(i,-1)}(\bullet\;;\;\bullet\;;\;),

(66)

where
\Omega^{i}(\mathbb{L}^{d})
is the set of
i
-cochains on
\mathbb{L}^{d}
. This definition is the same as we defined in equation (
20
). We can see
\delta_{j+1}\circ\delta_{j}=0
for
-1\leq j
.

4.3.2
Lattice de Rham coboundary operation

For
0\leq i\leq d-1
, we define
i
-th de Rham coboundary operation
d_{i}:C^{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\to C^{(i+1,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})
by

\begin{split}(d_{i}\omega)^{(i+1,j)}(x\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{i+1}\;;\;\bullet)&\\
=\frac{1}{(i+1)!}\sum_{\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{i+1}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)\sum_{k=1}^{i+1}\Biggl[(-1)^{k-1}\Bigl(&\omega^{(i,j)}(x+\hat{\mu}_{\tau(k)}\;;\;\mu_{\tau(1)},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{\tau(k)},\ldots,\mu_{\tau(i+1)}\;;\;\bullet)\\
-&\omega^{(i,j)}(x\;;\;\mu_{\tau(1)},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{\tau(k)},\ldots,\mu_{\tau(i+1)}\;;\;\bullet)\Bigr)\Biggr],\end{split}

(67)

where
\check{\mu}_{k}
means eliminating
\mu_{k}
.

Even for
i=-1
,
(-1)
-th de Rham coboundary operation
d_{-1}:C^{(-1,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\to C^{(0,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})
can be defined by

(d_{-1}\omega)^{(0,j)}(x\;;\;\;;\;\bullet)=\omega^{(-1,j)}(\;;\;\;;\;\bullet).

(68)

We can see
d_{d}=0
, and for
-1\leq i\leq d-1
, we find
d_{i+1}\circ d_{i}=0
.
In fact, in the case
i=d-1
,
d_{d}\circ d_{d-1}=0
is obviously true because
d_{d}=0
, and in the case
i=-1
,

\begin{split}(d_{0}d_{-1}\omega)^{(1,j)}(x\;;\;\mu\;;\;\bullet)&=(d_{-1}\omega)^{(0,j)}(x+\hat{\mu}\;;\;\;;\;\bullet)-(d_{-1}\omega)^{(0,j)}(x\;;\;\;;\;\bullet)\\
&=\omega^{(-1,j)}(\;;\;\;;\;\bullet)-\omega^{(-1,j)}(\;;\;\;;\;\bullet)=0\end{split}

(69)

holds, and in
0\leq i\leq d-2
, we can see
d_{i+1}\circ d_{i}=0
from

\begin{split}(d_{i+1}d_{i}\omega)^{(i+2,j)}(x\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,&\mu_{i+2}\;;\;\bullet)\\
=\text{antisym.}\sum_{k=1}^{i+2}\Biggl[(-1)^{k-1}\Bigl(&(d_{i}\omega)^{(i+1,j)}(x+\hat{\mu}_{k}\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{k},\ldots,\mu_{i+2}\;;\;\bullet)\\
-&(d_{i}\omega)^{(i+1,j)}(x\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{k},\ldots,\mu_{i+2}\;;\;\bullet)\Bigr)\Biggr]\\
=\text{antisym.}\sum_{k=1}^{i+2}\Biggl\{\sum_{1\leq l<k}\Biggl[(-1)^{l+k}\Bigl(&\omega^{(i,j)}(x+\hat{\mu}_{l}+\hat{\mu}_{k}\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{l},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{k},\ldots,\mu_{i+2}\;;\;\bullet)\\
-&\omega^{(i,j)}(x+\hat{\mu}_{k}\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{l},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{k},\ldots,\mu_{i+2}\;;\;\bullet)\\
-&\omega^{(i,j)}(x+\hat{\mu}_{l}\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{l},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{k},\ldots,\mu_{i+2}\;;\;\bullet)\\
+&\omega^{(i,j)}(x\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{l},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{k},\ldots,\mu_{i+2}\;;\;\bullet)\Bigr)\Biggr]\\
+\sum_{k<l\leq i+2}\Biggl[(-1)^{k+l-1}\Bigl(&\omega^{(i,j)}(x+\hat{\mu}_{k}+\hat{\mu}_{l}\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{k},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{l},\ldots,\mu_{i+2}\;;\;\bullet)\\
-&\omega^{(i,j)}(x+\hat{\mu}_{k}\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{k},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{l},\ldots,\mu_{i+2}\;;\;\bullet)\\
-&\omega^{(i,j)}(x+\hat{\mu}_{l}\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{k},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{l},\ldots,\mu_{i+2}\;;\;\bullet)\\
+&\omega^{(i,j)}(x\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{k},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{l},\ldots,\mu_{i+2}\;;\;\bullet)\Bigr)\Biggr]\Biggr\}\\
=\text{antisym.}\sum_{1\leq l<k\leq i+2}\Biggl[(-1)^{l+k}(1-1)\Bigl(&\omega^{(i,j)}(x+\hat{\mu}_{l}+\hat{\mu}_{k}\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{l},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{k},\ldots,\mu_{i+2}\;;\;\bullet)\\
-&\omega^{(i,j)}(x+\hat{\mu}_{k}\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{l},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{k},\ldots,\mu_{i+2}\;;\;\bullet)\\
-&\omega^{(i,j)}(x+\hat{\mu}_{l}\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{l},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{k},\ldots,\mu_{i+2}\;;\;\bullet)\\
+&\omega^{(i,j)}(x\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{l},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{k},\ldots,\mu_{i+2}\;;\;\bullet)\Bigr)\Biggr]\\
=0,&\end{split}

(70)

where “antisym.” denotes antisymmetrization.

4.3.3
Lattice DB coboundary operation

For integer
r
satisfying
0\leq r\leq d+1
, lattice Deligne–Beilinson cochain
C_{lat.\mathrm{DB}}^{r}(\mathbb{L}^{d})
of degree
r
9
9

9

It is by convention
i+j=r-1
rather than
i+j=r
.
can be defined by

C_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}^{r}(\mathbb{L}^{d}):=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=r-1\\
0\leq j\leq r\end{subarray}}C^{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d}).

(71)

For
0\leq r\leq d+1
, we define
C_{\mathrm{ev}}^{r}(\mathbb{L}^{d})
by

C_{\mathrm{ev}}^{r}(\mathbb{L}^{d}):=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{r}C^{(r-j-1,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d}).

(72)

Finally, we introduce lattice DB coboundary operations
D_{r}^{\mathrm{ev}},D_{r}
, given by

\displaystyle D_{r}^{\mathrm{ev}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{0}+\left[\sum_{k=1}^{r}\bigl(\delta_{k}+(-1)^{k}d_{r-k-1}\bigr)\right]

\displaystyle:C_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}^{r}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\to C_{\mathrm{ev}}^{r+1}(\mathbb{L}^{d}),

(73)

\displaystyle D_{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}(\delta_{k}+(-1)^{k+1}d_{r-k-2})

\displaystyle:C_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}^{r-1}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\to C_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}^{r}(\mathbb{L}^{d}).

(74)

5
Lattice DB cohomology

5.1
Lattice DB cycle/boundary

D
and
D^{\mathrm{ev}}
have a property
D^{\mathrm{ev}}\circ D=0
. We can directly prove it using
\delta_{j+1}\circ\delta_{j}=0
,
d_{i+1}\circ d_{i}=0
, and commutativity between
\delta
and
d
, as follows:

\begin{split}&D_{r}^{\mathrm{ev}}\circ D_{r}=(D_{r}^{\mathrm{ev}}:C^{r}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}\to C^{r}_{\mathrm{ev}})\circ(D_{r}:C^{r-1}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}\to C^{r}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}})\\
&=\left(\delta_{0}+\left[\sum_{k=1}^{r}\biggl(\delta_{k}+(-1)^{k}d_{r-k-1}\biggr)\right]\right)\left(\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\biggl(\delta_{k}+(-1)^{k+1}d_{r-k-1}\biggr)\right)\\
&=\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\delta_{k+1}\delta_{k}+\sum_{k=-1}^{r-2}d_{k+1}d_{k}+\left[\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\biggl((-1)^{k}d_{r-k-1}\delta_{k}+\delta_{k}(-1)^{k+1}d_{r-k-1}\biggr)\right]\\
&=0.\end{split}

(75)

As with the usual definition of cohomology theory, lattice Deligne–Beilinson cocycle/coboundary
Z_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}^{r}(\mathbb{L}^{d}),B_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}^{r}(\mathbb{L}^{d})
can be defined by

\begin{split}Z_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}^{r}(\mathbb{L}^{d}):=&\{\bm{\omega}\in C_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}^{r}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\mid D_{r}^{\mathrm{ev}}\bm{\omega}=0\},\\
B_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}^{r}(\mathbb{L}^{d}):=&D_{r}C_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}^{r-1}(\mathbb{L}^{d}),\end{split}

(76)

and we define lattice Deligne–Beilinson cohomology
H_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}^{r}(\mathbb{L}^{d})
by

H_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}^{r}(\mathbb{L}^{d}):=\frac{Z_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}^{r}(\mathbb{L}^{d})}{B_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}^{r}(\mathbb{L}^{d})}.

(77)

5.2
Properties of lattice DB cocycle

In this section, we describe basic properties of the lattice DB cocycle. In the following, let
\bm{\omega},\bm{\omega}^{\prime}
be

\begin{split}\bm{\omega}&=(\omega^{(r-1,0)},\omega^{(r-2,1)},\dots,\omega^{(-1,r)})\in Z^{r}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d}),\\
\bm{\omega}^{\prime}&=(\omega^{\prime(r-1,0)},\omega^{\prime(r-2,1)},\dots,\omega^{\prime(-1,r)})\in Z^{r}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d}).\end{split}

Lattice DB cocycles have the following properties:

•

(0,\dots,0)\in Z^{r}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d})

•

\bm{\omega},\bm{\omega}^{\prime}\in Z^{r}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d})

\Longrightarrow\bm{\omega}+\bm{\omega}^{\prime}:=(\omega^{(r-1,0)}+\omega^{\prime(r-1,0)},\omega^{(r-2,1)}+\omega^{\prime(r-2,1)},\dots,\omega^{(-1,r)}+\omega^{\prime(-1,r)})\in Z^{r}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d})

•

\omega^{(r-1,-1)}\in\Omega^{r-1}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\Longrightarrow(\delta_{-1}\omega^{(r-1,-1)},0,0,\dots,0)\in Z^{r}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d})

The proofs are simple and will be omitted.

An element of lattice DB cohomology can be multiplied by
\mathbb{Z}
, adding the same element an integer number of times, but in general it is not possible to multiply it by
\mathbb{R}
. However, as an exception,
\bm{\omega}
, realized as an image of
\Omega^{r-1}\hookrightarrow H_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}^{r}
, can be multiplied by
\mathbb{R}
in
\Omega^{r-1}
.

5.2.1
Global well-definedness of curvature

d_{r-1}\omega^{(r-1,0)}
can be considered as the curvature of
\bm{\omega}
. In the context of continuous Deligne–Beilinson
2
-cohomology, the curvature
d_{r-1}\omega^{(r-1,0)}
corresponds to the curvature of the continuous principal
U(1)
-bundle theory. For the reason that connections on a principal
U(1)
-bundle do not necessarily have zero curvature, the condition
d_{r-1}\omega^{(r-1,0)}=0
should not be imposed on lattice Deligne–Beilinson cochains. This is why we adopt
D^{\mathrm{ev}}_{r+1}\circ D_{r}=0
instead of
D_{r+1}\circ D_{r}=0
in the construction of the DB coboundary operation.

In the end, we describe that the curvature
d_{r-1}\omega^{(r-1,0)}
can be interpreted as a global
r
-form on lattice. First, since
\bm{\omega}
is a cocycle, it follows that

\delta\omega^{(r-1,0)}-d\omega^{(r-2,1)}=0.

(78)

Then we get

\delta(d_{r-1}\omega^{(r-1,0)})=\delta d\omega^{(r-1,0)}=d\delta\omega^{(r-1,0)}=0.

(79)

This means the values of
d_{r-1}\omega^{(r-1,0)}
are independent of the choice of
\tilde{\mathbb{L}}^{d}
indices. Then we conclude

\exists\omega^{(r,-1)}\in\Omega^{r}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\qquad\text{s.t.}d_{r-1}\omega^{(r-1,0)}=\delta_{-1}(\omega^{(r,-1)}).

(80)

We sometimes simply write
d\bm{\omega}
instead of
\omega^{(r,-1)}
.

5.2.2
Properties of
\omega^{(-1,r)}

Since
\delta\omega^{(0,r-1)}+(-1)^{r}d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,r)}=0
holds for any DB
r
-cocycle
\bm{\omega}
, we find

d_{-1}\delta_{r}\omega^{(-1,r)}=0,

(81)

and we can see
\delta_{r}\omega^{(-1,r)}=0
from the definition of
d_{-1}
.
10
10

10

For degrees other than
d_{-1}
, the condition
d\omega=0
does not necessarily imply
\omega=0
, and thus such an argument cannot be applied.
Therefore,
[\omega^{(-1,r)}]
can be considered to belong to Čech
r
-cohomology, and we can later observe that
[\omega^{(-1,r)}]
and
[\omega^{(r,-1)}]
are equivalent under the identification by isomorphism

(\text{\v{C}ech $r$-cohomology on $\mathbb{L}^{3}$})\cong H^{r}_{\text{de Rham}}(\mathbb{L}^{3}).

(82)

The lattice DB coboundary operator
D_{r}^{\mathrm{ev}}:C^{r}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}\to C^{r}_{\mathrm{ev}}
defined in (
73
), has a
\delta_{r}
term. Although the condition
D_{r}^{\mathrm{ev}}\bm{\omega}=0
implies
\delta_{r}\omega^{(-1,r)}=0
even when the
\delta_{r}
term is omitted, we keep the
\delta_{r}
term in
D^{\mathrm{ev}}_{r}
because it leads to a more convenient definition of the star product introduced in Section
6.5
.

6
Products of cochain

6.1
Čech cup product

Čech cup product of two Čech–de Rham cochains
\omega_{1}^{(-1,j_{1})},\omega_{2}^{(-1,j_{2})}
is written by

\omega_{1}^{(-1,j_{1})}\smallsmile\omega_{2}^{(-1,j_{2})}=(\omega_{1}\smallsmile\omega_{2})^{(-1,j_{1}+j_{2})}.

(83)

In the following, we denote
\omega^{(-1,j)}(;;\tilde{x}^{(0)},\dotsc,\tilde{x}^{(j)})
by
\omega(0,\dotsc,j)
as abbreviation. Under this notation, let the product
\omega_{1}\smallsmile\omega_{2}
be defined by

(\omega_{1}\smallsmile\omega_{2})(0,\dotsc,j_{1}+j_{2})=\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\omega_{1}(\sigma(0),\dotsc,\sigma(j_{1}))\omega_{2}(\sigma(j_{1}),\dotsc,\sigma(j_{1}+j_{2})).

(84)

Note that
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{j_{1}+j_{2}+1}
is a unique permutation that satisfies
\tilde{x}^{(\sigma(0))}\prec\tilde{x}^{(\sigma(1))}\prec\cdots
, where
\prec
denotes the total ordering
11
11

11

The total ordering of simplicial complex, sometimes called the branching structure, is defined by arbitrarily choosing and fixing the total order of
\tilde{\mathbb{L}}^{d}
.
of patch indices. It should be emphasized that
\omega_{1}\smallsmile\omega_{2}
is defined antisymmetrically with respect to the patch indices.

6.2
Čech cup-
1
product

The Čech cup product defined in Section
6.1
is graded non-commutative at the cochain level. The non-commutativity is expressed by the Čech cup-
1
product
Steenrod:1947
. In the following, we will use the same abbreviation as in Section
6.1
.

For
0\leq j_{1}\;,\;0\leq j_{2}\;,\;1\leq j_{1}+j_{2}
, Čech cup-
1
product of
\omega_{1}^{(-1,j_{1})}
and
\omega_{2}^{(-1,j_{2})}
is defined by

\begin{split}&(\omega_{1}\smallsmile_{1}\omega_{2})(0,\dotsc,j_{1}+j_{2}-1)\\
=&\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\sum_{0\leq k\leq j_{1}-1}\omega_{1}(\sigma(0),\dotsc,\sigma(k),\sigma(k+j_{2}),\dotsc,\sigma(j_{1}+j_{2}-1))\omega_{2}(\sigma(k),\dotsc,\sigma(k+j_{2})),\end{split}

(85)

where
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{j_{1}+j_{2}}
is the same as in Section
6.1
.

The coboundary formula, which is an important formula of the cup-
1
product

\begin{split}&\omega_{1}^{(-1,j_{1})}\smallsmile\omega_{2}^{(-1,j_{2})}-(-1)^{j_{1}j_{2}}\omega_{2}^{(-1,j_{2})}\smallsmile\omega_{1}^{(-1,j_{1})}\\
=&\delta(\omega_{1}^{(-1,j_{1})}\smallsmile_{1}\omega_{2}^{(-1,j_{2})})+(\delta\omega_{1}^{(-1,j_{1})})\smallsmile_{1}\omega_{2}^{(-1,j_{2})}+(-1)^{j_{1}}\omega_{1}^{(-1,j_{1})}\smallsmile_{1}(\delta\omega_{2}^{(-1,j_{2})})\end{split}

(86)

is known
Mosher:2008
.

6.3
Lattice wedge product

Lattice wedge product of two Čech–de Rham cochains
\omega_{1}^{(i_{1},-1)},\omega_{2}^{(i_{2},-1)}
is written by

\omega_{1}^{(i_{1},-1)}\wedge\omega_{2}^{(i_{2},-1)}=(\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2})^{(i_{1}+i_{2},-1)}.

(87)

In the following, we denote
\omega^{(i,-1)}(x;\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{i};)
by
\omega(x;1,\dots,i)
as abbreviation. Under this notation, let the product
\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}
be defined by

\begin{split}&(j_{1}!j_{2}!)(\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2})(x;1,\dotsc,i_{1}+i_{2})\\
=&\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{j_{1}+j_{2}}}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\omega_{1}(x;\sigma(1),\dotsc,\sigma(j_{1}))\omega_{2}(x+\hat{\sigma}(1)+\cdots+\hat{\sigma}(j_{1});\sigma(j_{1}+1),\dotsc,\sigma(j_{1}+j_{2})).\end{split}

(88)

Note that
\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}
is defined antisymmetrically with respect to the indices
1,\dotsc,i_{1}+i_{2}
.

6.4
Čech–de Rham product

Using
\smallsmile
and
\wedge
, we define products of Čech–de Rham chain
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}
.

6.4.1
Definition of
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}

For two Čech–de Rham cochains

\displaystyle\omega^{(i,j)}(x\;;\;\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{i}\;;\;\tilde{x}^{(0)},\dots,\tilde{x}^{(j)})

\displaystyle\eta^{(k,l)}(x\;;\;\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{k}\;;\;\tilde{y}^{(0)},\dots,\tilde{y}^{(l)}),

(89)

let

\omega\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\;\eta\in C^{(i+k,j+l)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})

(90)

be

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\omega\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\,\eta)^{(i+k,j+l)}(x\;;\;\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{i+k}\;;\;\tilde{x}^{(0)},\dots,\tilde{x}^{(j+l)})\\
:=&\frac{1}{i!k!}\sum_{\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{i+k}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)\omega(x\;;\;\mu_{\tau(1)},\dots,\mu_{\tau(i)}\;;\;\tilde{x}^{\sigma(0)},\dots,\tilde{x}^{\sigma(j)})\\
&\eta(x+\hat{\mu}_{\tau(1)}+\dots+\hat{\mu}_{\tau(i)}\;;\;\mu_{\tau(i+1)},\dots,\mu_{\tau(i+k)}\;;\;\tilde{x}^{\sigma(j)},\dots,\tilde{x}^{\sigma(j+l)})\\
=&\frac{1}{i!k!}\sum_{\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{i+k}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)\Biggl.\Bigl(\omega(x\;;\;\mu_{\tau(1)},\dots,\mu_{\tau(i)}\;;\;-)\\
&\smallsmile\eta(x+\hat{\mu}_{\tau(1)}+\dots+\hat{\mu}_{\tau(i)}\;;\;\mu_{\tau(i+1)},\dots,\mu_{\tau(i+k)}\;;\;-)\Bigr)\Biggr|_{(\tilde{x}^{(0)},\dots,\tilde{x}^{(j+l-1)})}\\
=&\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\Biggl.\Bigl(\omega(-\;;\;-\;;\;\tilde{x}^{\sigma(0)},\dots,\tilde{x}^{\sigma(j)})\wedge\eta(-\;;\;-\;;\;\tilde{x}^{\sigma(j)},\dots,\tilde{x}^{\sigma(j+l)})\Bigr)\Biggr|_{(x\;;\;\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{i+k-1})},\end{split}

(91)

where
\omega^{(i,j)}(-;-;\bullet)
means to regard
\omega^{(i,j)}
as
\omega^{(i,-1)}
substituting only for the
\bullet
part. Similarly,
\omega^{(i,j)}(\bullet;\bullet;-)
means to regard
\omega^{(i,j)}
as
\omega^{(-1,j)}
substituting only for
\bullet
part.

6.4.2
Leibniz rule of
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}
is defined as a composite operation of
\smallsmile
,
\wedge
, and substituting. Therefore, Leibniz rule of
\delta,d
can also be applied to
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}},
then we obtain

\displaystyle\delta(\omega^{(i,j)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\eta^{(k,l)})=(\delta\omega^{(i,j)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\eta^{(k,l)}+(-1)^{j}\omega^{(i,j)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}(\delta\eta^{(k,l)}),

(92)

\displaystyle d(\omega^{(i,j)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\eta^{(k,l)})=(d\omega^{(i,j)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\eta^{(k,l)}+(-1)^{i}\omega^{(i,j)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}(d\eta^{(k,l)}).

(93)

6.5
Definition of the lattice DB star product

For integer
a,b
satisfies
0\leq a\leq b\leq r
, we define the direct sum of Čech–de Rham cochain
C^{r}_{[a,b]}(\mathbb{L}^{d})
by

C^{r}_{[a,b]}(\mathbb{L}^{d}):=\bigoplus_{0\leq a\leq k\leq b\leq r}C^{(r-k-1,k)}(\mathbb{L}^{d}).

(94)

The relationship with the notation we have used is

\displaystyle C^{r}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d})=C^{r}_{[0,r]}(\mathbb{L}^{d}),

\displaystyle C^{r}_{\mathrm{ev}}(\mathbb{L}^{d})=C^{r}_{[1,r]}(\mathbb{L}^{d}).

(95)

Next, we will give the definition of
\bm{\omega}\star\bm{\eta}\in C_{[s+u,t+v]}^{p+q}(\mathbb{L}^{d})
for

\begin{split}\bm{\omega}=(\omega^{(p-s-1,s)},\omega^{(p-s-2,s+1)},\dots,\omega^{(p-t,t-1)},\omega^{(p-t-1,t)})\in C^{p}_{[s,t]}(\mathbb{L}^{d}),\\
\bm{\eta}=(\eta^{(q-u-1,u)},\eta^{(q-u-2,u+1)},\dots,\eta^{(q-v,v-1)},\eta^{(q-v-1,v)})\in C^{q}_{[u,v]}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\end{split}

(96)

by

\begin{split}&\bm{\omega}\star\bm{\eta}=\\
&\begin{array}[]{rrlcrll}(&&\omega^{(p-s-1,s)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&d_{q-u-1}&\eta^{(q-u-1,u)}&,\\
&&\omega^{(p-s-2,s+1)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&d_{q-u-1}&\eta^{(q-u-1,u)}&,\\
&&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\\
&&\omega^{(p-t,t-1)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&d_{q-u-1}&\eta^{(q-u-1,u)}&,\\
&d_{p-t-1}&\omega^{(p-t-1,t)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&&\eta^{(q-u-1,u)}&,\\
&d_{p-t-1}&\omega^{(p-t-1,t)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&&\eta^{(q-u-2,u+1)}&,\\
&&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\\
&d_{p-t-1}&\omega^{(p-t-1,t)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&&\eta^{(q-v,v-1)}&,\\
&d_{p-t-1}&\omega^{(p-t-1,t)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&&\eta^{(q-v-1,v)}&\;).\end{array}\end{split}

(97)

From the definition, we can see

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{2pt}}c@{\hspace{2pt}}lcccccl}C^{a}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\hfil\hskip 2.0&\star\hfil\hskip 2.0&C^{b}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d})&=&C^{a}_{[0,a]}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\star C^{b}_{[0,b]}(\mathbb{L}^{d})&=&C^{a+b}_{[0,a+b]}(\mathbb{L}^{d})&=&C^{a+b}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d}),\\
C^{a}_{\mathrm{ev}}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\hfil\hskip 2.0&\star\hfil\hskip 2.0&C^{b}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d})&=&C^{a}_{[1,a]}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\star C^{b}_{[0,b]}(\mathbb{L}^{d})&=&C^{a+b}_{[1,a+b]}(\mathbb{L}^{d})&=&C^{a+b}_{\mathrm{ev}}(\mathbb{L}^{d}),\\
C^{a}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\hfil\hskip 2.0&\star\hfil\hskip 2.0&C^{b}_{\mathrm{ev}}(\mathbb{L}^{d})&=&C^{a}_{[0,a]}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\star C^{b}_{[1,b]}(\mathbb{L}^{d})&=&C^{a+b}_{[1,a+b]}(\mathbb{L}^{d})&=&C^{a+b}_{\mathrm{ev}}(\mathbb{L}^{d}).\end{array}

(98)

Below, we will introduce the properties of
\star
without proof, which will be provided in Section
6.6
.
\star
satisfies

•

\forall\bm{\omega},\forall\bm{\omega}^{\prime}\in C^{a}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d}),\forall\bm{\eta}\in C^{b}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\;\;,\;(\bm{\omega}+\bm{\omega}^{\prime})\star\bm{\eta}=\bm{\omega}\star\bm{\eta}+\bm{\omega}^{\prime}\star\bm{\eta}

•

\forall\bm{\omega}\in C^{a}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d}),\forall\bm{\eta}\in C^{b}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\;\;,\;D_{a+b}(\bm{\omega}\star\bm{\eta})=(D_{a}\bm{\omega})\star\bm{\eta}+(-1)^{a}\bm{\omega}\star(D_{b}\bm{\eta})

on DB cochain, and satisfies

•

\forall\bm{\omega}\in Z^{a}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d}),\forall\bm{\eta}\in Z^{b}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\;\;,\;\bm{\omega}\star\bm{\eta}\in Z^{a+b}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d})

•

\forall\bm{\omega},\forall\bm{\omega}^{\prime}\in Z^{a}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d}),\forall\bm{\eta}\in Z^{b}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\;\;,\;[\bm{\omega}]=[\bm{\omega}^{\prime}]\Longrightarrow[\bm{\omega}\star\bm{\eta}]=[\bm{\omega}^{\prime}\star\bm{\eta}]

on DB cocycle.

From the above properties, we can see that
\star
is formulated as a product on the lattice DB cohomology; however, it is not graded commutative.

6.6
Proofs of the properties of
\star

Below, we consider the case

\begin{split}\bm{\omega}=(\omega^{(a-1,0)},\omega^{(a-2,1)},\dots,\omega^{(0,a-1)},\omega^{(-1,a)},)\in C^{a}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\\
\bm{\eta}=(\eta^{(b-1,0)},\eta^{(b-2,1)},\dots,\eta^{(0,b-1)},\eta^{(-1,b)})\in C^{b}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}(\mathbb{L}^{d}).\end{split}

(99)

6.6.1
Proof of
(\boldsymbol{\omega}+\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime})\star\boldsymbol{\eta}=\boldsymbol{\omega}\star\boldsymbol{\eta}+\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}\star\boldsymbol{\eta}

It is obvious from the linearity of
d,\smallsmile,\wedge
.

6.6.2
Proof of
D_{a+b}^{\mathrm{ev}}(\boldsymbol{\omega}\star\boldsymbol{\eta})=(D_{a}^{\mathrm{ev}}\boldsymbol{\omega})\star\boldsymbol{\eta}+(-1)^{a}\boldsymbol{\omega}\star(D_{b}^{\mathrm{ev}}\boldsymbol{\eta})

This is proved by the following direct calculation:
12
12

12

The sizes of parentheses in DB cochain expressions are adjusted for typographical clarity and carry no additional meaning.

(D_{a}^{\mathrm{ev}}\bm{\omega})\star\bm{\eta}=\left(\begin{array}[]{rrl}(\delta_{0}\omega^{(a-1,0)}+(-1)^{1}d_{a-2}\omega^{(a-2,1)})&,\\
\vdots&,\\
(\delta_{a-2}\omega^{(1,a-2)}+(-1)^{a-1}d_{0}\omega^{(0,a-1)})&,\\
(\delta_{a-1}\omega^{(0,a-1)}+(-1)^{a}d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)})&,\\
d_{-1}\delta_{a}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\end{array}\right)\star\left(\begin{array}[]{rll}\eta^{(b-1,0)}&,\\
\eta^{(b-2,1)}&,\\
\vdots&,\\
\eta^{(0,b-1)}&,\\
\eta^{(-1,b)}&\end{array}\right)

\begin{split}\begin{array}[]{rrcr}=\Bigl(&(\delta_{0}\omega^{(a-1,0)}+(-1)^{1}d_{a-2}\omega^{(a-2,1)})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&d_{b-1}\eta^{(b-1,0)},\\
&\vdots&&,\\
&(\delta_{a-2}\omega^{(1,a-2)}+(-1)^{a-1}d_{0}\omega^{(0,a-1)})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&d_{b-1}\eta^{(b-1,0)},\\
&(\delta_{a-1}\omega^{(0,a-1)}+(-1)^{a}d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&d_{b-1}\eta^{(b-1,0)},\\
&d_{-1}\delta_{a}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&\eta^{(b-1,0)},\\
&\vdots&&,\\
&d_{-1}\delta_{a}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&\eta^{(0,b-1)},\\
&d_{-1}\delta_{a}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&\eta^{(-1,b)}\Bigr),\end{array}\end{split}

(100)

\begin{split}\bm{\omega}\star(D_{b}^{\mathrm{ev}}\bm{\eta})=\left(\begin{array}[]{rll}\omega^{(a-1,0)}&,\\
\omega^{(a-2,1)}&,\\
\vdots&,\\
\omega^{(0,a-1)}&,\\
\omega^{(-1,a)}&\end{array}\right)\star\left(\begin{array}[]{rrl}(\delta_{0}\eta^{(b-1,0)}+(-1)^{1}d_{b-2}\eta^{(b-2,1)})&,\\
\vdots&,\\
(\delta_{b-2}\eta^{(1,b-2)}+(-1)^{a-1}d_{0}\eta^{(0,b-1)})&,\\
(\delta_{b-1}\eta^{(0,b-1)}+(-1)^{a}d_{-1}\eta^{(-1,b)})&,\\
d_{-1}\delta_{b}\eta^{(-1,b)}&\end{array}\right)\\
\begin{array}[]{rrcr}=\Bigl(&\omega^{(a-1,0)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&d_{b-1}\delta_{0}\eta^{(b-1,0)},\\
&\omega^{(a-2,1)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&d_{b-1}\delta_{0}\eta^{(b-1,0)},\\
&\vdots&&,\\
&\omega^{(0,a-1)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&d_{b-1}\delta_{0}\eta^{(b-1,0)},\\
&d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&(\delta_{0}\eta^{(b-1,0)}+(-1)^{1}d_{b-2}\eta^{(b-2,1)}),\\
&d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&(\delta_{0}\eta^{(b-2,1)}+(-1)^{2}d_{b-3}\eta^{(b-3,2)}),\\
&\vdots&&,\\
&d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&(\delta_{b-1}\eta^{(0,b-1)}+(-1)^{b}d_{-1}\eta^{(-1,b)}),\\
&d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&\delta_{b}\eta^{(-1,b)}\Bigr),\end{array}\end{split}

(101)

\begin{array}[]{rrcr}D_{a+b}^{\mathrm{ev}}(\bm{\omega}\star\bm{\eta})=\Bigl(&\qquad\quad\delta_{0}(\omega^{(a-1,0)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&d_{b-1}\eta^{(b-1,0)})\\
+&(-1)^{1}d_{a+b-2}(\omega^{(a-2,1)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&d_{b-1}\eta^{(b-1,0)}),\\
&&\vdots&,\\
&\delta_{a-2}(\omega^{(1,a-2)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&d_{b-1}\eta^{(b-1,0)})\\
+&(-1)^{a-1}d_{b}(\omega^{(0,a-1)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&d_{b-1}\eta^{(b-1,0)}),\\
&\delta_{a-1}(\omega^{(0,a-1)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&d_{b-1}\eta^{(b-1,0)})\\
+&(-1)^{a}d_{b-1}(d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&\eta^{(b-1,0)}),\\
&\delta_{a}(d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&\eta^{(b-1,0)})\\
+&(-1)^{a+1}d_{b-2}(d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&\eta^{(b-2,1)}),\\
&&\vdots&,\\
&\delta_{a+b-1}(d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&\eta^{(0,b-1)})\\
+&(-1)^{a+b}d_{-1}(d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&\eta^{(-1,b)}),\\
&\delta_{a+b}(d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&\eta^{(-1,b)})\Bigr)\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rcr}=\Bigl((\delta_{0}\omega^{(a-1,0)}+(-1)^{1}d_{a-2}\omega^{(a-2,1)})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&d_{b-1}\eta^{(b-1,0)}\\
+(-1)^{0}\omega^{(a-2,1)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&\delta_{0}d_{b-1}\eta^{(b-1,0)},\\
&\vdots&,\\
(\delta_{a-2}\omega^{(1,a-2)}+(-1)^{a-1}d_{0}\omega^{(0,a-1)})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&d_{b-1}\eta^{(b-1,0)}\\
+(-1)^{a-2}\omega^{(1,a-2)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&\delta_{0}d_{b-1}\eta^{(b-1,0)},\\
(\delta_{a-1}\omega^{(0,a-1)}+(-1)^{a}d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&d_{b-1}\eta^{(b-1,0)}\\
+(-1)^{a-1}\omega^{(0,a-1)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&\delta_{0}d_{b-1}\eta^{(b-1,0)},\\
\delta_{a}d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&\eta^{(b-1,0)}\\
+d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&(-1)^{a}(\delta_{0}\eta^{(b-1,0)}+(-1)^{1}d_{b-2}\eta^{(b-2,1)}),\\
&\vdots&,\\
\delta_{a}d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&\eta^{(0,b-1)}\\
+d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&(-1)^{a}(\delta_{b-1}\eta^{(0,b-1)}+(-1)^{b}d_{-1}\eta^{(-1,b)}),\\
\delta_{a}d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&\eta^{(-1,b)}\\
+d_{-1}\omega^{(-1,a)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}&(-1)^{a}\delta_{b}\eta^{(-1,b)}\Bigr)\\
=(D_{a}^{\mathrm{ev}}\bm{\omega})\star\bm{\eta}+(-1)^{a}\bm{\omega}\star(D_{b}^{\mathrm{ev}}\bm{\eta}).&&\end{array}

(102)

6.6.3
Proof of
\forall\boldsymbol{\omega}\in Z_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}\;,\;\boldsymbol{\eta}\in Z_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}\Longrightarrow\boldsymbol{\omega}\star\boldsymbol{\eta}\in Z_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}

Recall Section
6.6.2
. In the case
\bm{\omega}\in Z_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}\;,\;\bm{\eta}\in Z_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}
, the RHS of

D^{\mathrm{ev}}(\bm{\omega}\star\bm{\eta})=(D^{\mathrm{ev}}\bm{\omega})\star\bm{\eta}\pm\bm{\omega}\star(D^{\mathrm{ev}}\bm{\eta})

(103)

equals to
0
. Then
\bm{\omega}\star\bm{\eta}\in Z_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}
is concluded.

6.6.4
Proof of
\forall\boldsymbol{\omega}\in Z^{a}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}},\;\forall\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}\in Z^{a}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}},\;\forall\boldsymbol{\eta}\in Z^{b}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}
,
[\boldsymbol{\omega}]=[\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}]\Longrightarrow[\boldsymbol{\omega}\star\boldsymbol{\eta}]=[\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}\star\boldsymbol{\eta}]

By definition,

[\bm{\omega}]=[\bm{\omega}^{\prime}]\Longleftrightarrow\exists\bm{\xi}\in C^{a-1}_{\mathrm{lat.DB}}\;\mathrm{s.t.}\;D_{a}\bm{\xi}=\bm{\omega}-\bm{\omega}^{\prime}

(104)

holds.
\bm{\xi}
in this formula holds

\begin{split}&D_{a+b}(\bm{\xi}\star\bm{\eta})=(d_{a+b-1}+D^{\mathrm{ev}}_{a+b-1})(\bm{\xi}\star\bm{\eta})\\
=&d_{a-2}\xi^{(a-2,0)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}d_{b-1}\eta^{(b-1,0)}+(D_{a-1}^{\mathrm{ev}}\bm{\xi})\star\bm{\eta}\pm\bm{\eta}\star(D_{b}^{\mathrm{ev}}\bm{\eta})\\
=&(D_{a}\bm{\xi})\star\bm{\eta}\pm\bm{\eta}\star(D_{b}^{\mathrm{ev}}\bm{\eta})=\bm{\omega}\star\bm{\eta}-\bm{\omega}^{\prime}\star\bm{\eta}.\end{split}

(105)

Therefore,
[\bm{\omega}\star\bm{\eta}]=[\bm{\omega}^{\prime}\star\bm{\eta}]
holds.

6.7
Two equivalent definitions of lattice DB star product

The first component of the definition of
\star
discussed so far has the form
AdB
, but for future discussions, we would also like to define a product whose first component has the form
dAB
. We denote each different operations by
\bm{\omega}\overrightarrow{\underleftarrow{\star}}\bm{\eta},\bm{\omega}\overleftarrow{\underrightarrow{\star}}\bm{\eta}
. As we will see shortly, we can see
[\bm{\omega}\overrightarrow{\underleftarrow{\star}}\bm{\eta}]=[\bm{\omega}\overleftarrow{\underrightarrow{\star}}\bm{\eta}]
at least the case of degree
2
, therefore these definitions are gauge equivalent within the framework of lattice DB
2
-cohomology.

We introduce notations
\overrightarrow{\underleftarrow{\star}}=\star,
and
\overleftarrow{\underrightarrow{\star}}
by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\bm{\omega}\overrightarrow{\underleftarrow{\star}}\bm{\eta}=\bm{\omega}\star\bm{\eta}=\\
&\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{2pt}}r@{\hspace{2pt}}l@{\hspace{2pt}}c@{\hspace{2pt}}r@{\hspace{2pt}}l@{\hspace{2pt}}l}(\hskip 2.0&\hskip 2.0&\omega^{(a-1,0)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&d_{b-1}\hskip 2.0&\eta^{(a-1,0)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&,\\
\hskip 2.0&\hskip 2.0&\omega^{(a-2,1)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&d_{b-1}\hskip 2.0&\eta^{(a-1,0)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&,\\
\hskip 2.0&\hskip 2.0&\vdots\hfil\hskip 2.0&\vdots\hfil\hskip 2.0&\hskip 2.0&\vdots\hfil\hskip 2.0&\\
\hskip 2.0&\hskip 2.0&\omega^{(0,a-1)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&d_{b-1}\hskip 2.0&\eta^{(a-1,0)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&,\\
\hskip 2.0&d_{-1}\hskip 2.0&\omega^{(-1,a)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\hskip 2.0&\eta^{(a-1,0)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&,\\
\hskip 2.0&d_{-1}\hskip 2.0&\omega^{(-1,a)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\hskip 2.0&\eta^{(a-2,1)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&,\\
\hskip 2.0&\hskip 2.0&\vdots\hfil\hskip 2.0&\vdots\hfil\hskip 2.0&\hskip 2.0&\vdots\hfil\hskip 2.0&\\
\hskip 2.0&d_{-1}\hskip 2.0&\omega^{(-1,a)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\hskip 2.0&\eta^{(0,a-1)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&,\\
\hskip 2.0&d_{-1}\hskip 2.0&\omega^{(-1,a)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\hskip 2.0&\eta^{(-1,a)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\;),\end{array}\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\bm{\omega}\overleftarrow{\underrightarrow{\star}}\bm{\eta}=\\
&\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{2pt}}l@{\hspace{2pt}}l@{\hspace{2pt}}l@{\hspace{2pt}}c@{\hspace{2pt}}r@{\hspace{2pt}}l@{\hspace{2pt}}l}(\hskip 2.0&(-1)^{a}\hfil\hskip 2.0&d_{a-1}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\omega^{(a-1,0)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\hskip 2.0&\eta^{(a-1,0)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&,\\
\hskip 2.0&(-1)^{2a}\hfil\hskip 2.0&d_{a-1}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\omega^{(a-1,0)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\hskip 2.0&\eta^{(a-2,1)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&,\\
\hskip 2.0&\;\;\;\vdots\hfil\hskip 2.0&\hfil\hskip 2.0&\vdots\hfil\hskip 2.0&\vdots\hfil\hskip 2.0&\hskip 2.0&\vdots\hfil\hskip 2.0&\\
\hskip 2.0&(-1)^{a^{2}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&d_{a-1}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\omega^{(a-1,0)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\hskip 2.0&\eta^{(0,a-1)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&,\\
\hskip 2.0&(-1)^{a^{2}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\hfil\hskip 2.0&\omega^{(a-1,0)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&d_{-1}\hskip 2.0&\eta^{(-1,a)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&,\\
\hskip 2.0&(-1)^{(a-1)a}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\hfil\hskip 2.0&\omega^{(a-2,1)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&d_{-1}\hskip 2.0&\eta^{(-1,a)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&,\\
\hskip 2.0&\hfil\hskip 2.0&\hfil\hskip 2.0&\vdots\hfil\hskip 2.0&\vdots\hfil\hskip 2.0&\hskip 2.0&\vdots\hfil\hskip 2.0&\\
\hskip 2.0&(-1)^{a}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\hfil\hskip 2.0&\omega^{(0,a-1)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&d_{-1}\hskip 2.0&\eta^{(-1,a)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&,\\
\hskip 2.0&(-1)^{0}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\hfil\hskip 2.0&\omega^{(-1,a)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&d_{-1}\hskip 2.0&\eta^{(-1,a)}\hfil\hskip 2.0&\;).\end{array}\end{split}

(106)

In the case of
a=2
,

\begin{split}&(A,\Lambda,n)\overrightarrow{\underleftarrow{\star}}(B,\Pi,m)-(A,\Lambda,n)\overleftarrow{\underrightarrow{\star}}(B,\Pi,m)\\
=&\left(\begin{array}[]{c}A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}dB-(-1)^{2}dA\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}B,\\
\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}dB-(-1)^{4}dA\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\Pi,\\
dn\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}B-(-1)^{4}A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}dm,\\
dn\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\Pi-(-1)^{2}\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}dm,\\
dn\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}m-(-1)^{0}n\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}dm\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}dB-dA\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}B,\\
\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}dB-dA\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\Pi,\\
dn\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}B-A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}dm,\\
dn\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\Pi-\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}dm,\\
dn\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}m-n\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}dm\end{array}\right)\end{split}

(107)

\begin{split}=&\left(\begin{array}[]{c}A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}dB-dA\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}B,\\
\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}dB-dA\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\Pi,\\
dn\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}B-A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}dm,\\
dn\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\Pi-\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}dm,\\
0\end{array}\right)=D_{4}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}B,\\
-\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}B+A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\Pi,\\
-\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smallsmile}}{{\wedge}}\Pi,\\
0\end{array}\right)\end{split}

(108)

holds. The important thing here is that the fourth component of the RHS of the equation is
0
. As we will see later in (
135
), the integral of DB cohomology has a modulo
\mathbb{Z}
ambiguity depending on the DB boundary. However, since the fourth component is
0
, formula

\int_{\mathrm{DBcycle}}(A,\Lambda,n)\overrightarrow{\underleftarrow{\star}}(B,\Pi,m)=\int_{\mathrm{DBcycle}}(A,\Lambda,n)\overleftarrow{\underrightarrow{\star}}(B,\Pi,m)

(109)

exactly hold. This is important in the discussion of linking numbers that appears in Section
9
.

7
Lattice DB cycle and integration

We move on to the integral of a lattice DB cocycle. The lattice DB cycle introduced in this section corresponds to the integral domain. As we already defined the lattice DB chain
C_{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})
in (
57
), we are going to define the lattice DB cycle via a boundary operation on the DB chain, given as a dual of the coboundary operation. By defining the DB cycle, the gauge-invariant
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
-valued integral is well-defined, and Stokes’ theorem for lattice DB cohomology also holds. Finally, we will show concrete examples of the lattice DB cycle.

7.1
Boundary operations

Let us review the coboundary operators
d
and
\delta
on lattice DB cochains:

(\delta_{j}\omega)^{(i,j+1)}(\bullet\;;\;\bullet\;;\;\tilde{x}^{(0)},\ldots,\tilde{x}^{(j+1)})=\sum_{k=0}^{j+1}(-1)^{k}\omega^{(i,j)}(\bullet\;;\;\bullet\;;\;\tilde{x}^{(0)},\ldots,\check{\tilde{x}}^{(k)},\ldots,\tilde{x}^{(j+1)}),

(110)

\begin{split}(d_{i}\omega)^{(i+1,j)}(x\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{i+1}\;;\;\bullet)\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad&\\
=\text{antisym.}\sum_{k=1}^{i+1}\Biggl[(-1)^{k-1}\Bigl(\omega^{(i,j)}(x+\hat{\mu}_{k}\;;\;&\mu_{1},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{k},\ldots,\mu_{i+1}\;;\;\bullet)\\
-\omega^{(i,j)}(x\;;\;&\mu_{1},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{k},\ldots,\mu_{i+1}\;;\;\bullet)\Bigr)\Biggr].\end{split}

(111)

We define dual operations of
d,\delta
by

\Delta_{j+1}(\bullet\;;\;\bullet\;;\;\tilde{x}^{(0)},\ldots,\tilde{x}^{(j+1)})=\sum_{k=0}^{j+1}(-1)^{k}(\bullet\;;\;\bullet\;;\;\tilde{x}^{(0)},\ldots,\check{\tilde{x}}^{(k)},\ldots,\tilde{x}^{(j+1)}),

(112)

\begin{split}\partial_{i+1}(x\;;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{i+1}\;;\;\bullet)=\text{antisym.}\sum_{k=1}^{i+1}\Biggl[(-1)^{k-1}\Bigl((x+\hat{\mu}_{k}\;&;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{k},\ldots,\mu_{i+1}\;;\;\bullet)\\
-(x\;&;\;\mu_{1},\ldots,\check{\mu}_{k},\ldots,\mu_{i+1}\;;\;\bullet)\Bigr)\Biggr].\end{split}

(113)

Expanding the operations
\mathbb{Z}
-linearly, we get

\begin{split}\Delta_{j+1}:C_{(i,j+1)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\to C_{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d}),\\
\partial_{i+1}:C_{(i+1,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d})\to C_{(i,j)}(\mathbb{L}^{d}).\end{split}

(114)

7.2
Natural pairing and duality formula

Let us define the integral as a natural pairing of a lattice DB cochain and a lattice DB chain.

Since we defined lattice Čech–de Rham cochain of degree
(i,j)
by
